We propose JANUS, a red-blue adversarial framework for automated cross-layer defense synthesis. Starting from a known exploit, \sys iteratively refines deployment-specific policies in an isolated offline environment. A black-box attacker probes the current policy for exploit variants using only externally observable feedback. The defender summarizes each verified variant into its security-relevant effects, uses counterfactual replay to separate complementary coverage from redundant enforcement, and jointly selects and scopes controls across layers. Each candidate policy is validated against accumulated exploits and benign workflows. We evaluate JANUS on sixteen vulnerability deployments across six Java and PHP applications against gateway, application, system, and cross-layer defenses. JANUS reduces held-out attack success to 6.2%, compared with 18.8-55.0% for the baselines, fully mitigates 13 of 16 deployments, and preserves 98.4% of benign functionality tests at a 6.9% targeted P95 latency increase.


## arXiv 2610.35530v1 - CANS: Capability-Preserving Neuron Steering for Large Language Models

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T14:45:23Z
- authors: Yucheng Wu, Chengyi Xie, Chenpeng Wang, Liangming Pan
- query-set: cs.AI

Activation steering offers training-free and dynamic control of target behaviors in large language models, but existing methods can impair general capabilities and require domain-specific intervention designs. We introduce Capability-Preserving Neuron Steering (CANS), a unified framework that balances target-behavior control with capability preservation. To make target-behavior improvement and general-capability preservation tractable, we introduce two proxies: log-probabilities of target tokens in model generated responses, and output-distribution KL divergence on general-purpose inputs. Combining the local approximation of the two proxies yields a closed-form solution, enabling lightweight steering without iterative model training or additional inference-time computation. The resulting neuron-wise scaling coefficients achieve effective steering while limiting general-capability loss. A shared data-construction and intervention procedure supports adaptation across domains using sampled responses with target-behavior filtering and target-span annotations. Across safety, toxicity, and hallucination control on Qwen and Llama models, CANS achieves comparable or better steering while better preserving MMLU, BBH, and IFEval performance than SFT, CAA, and neuron-level baselines. On Qwen3-4B, CANS reduces HarmBench attack success from $15.50\%$ to $1.75\%$ with a $1.11$-point decrease in average capability. These results support CANS as a practical approach to behavioral control across domains.


## arXiv 2610.35523v1 - Ranking Function Generation with LLMs for Termination

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.LO
- published: 2026-10-26T14:41:52Z
- authors: Rongkang Li, Jiaxin Hu, Taolue Chen, Yong Li, Fu Song
- query-set: cs.AI

Ranking functions are a fundamental technique for proving loop termination, but constructing them manually is challenging and requires substantial expertise. We propose RAINFALL, a neuro-symbolic framework which follows the generate-verify-refine paradigm and tightly integrates LLMs with symbolic verification. RAINFALL formulates ranking function synthesis as a code-completion task by inserting placeholders into the input program and constraining the LLM to fill four predefined slots for each loop. The completed program is then checked by a symbolic verifier. If verification succeeds, a valid ranking function is obtained; otherwise, the verifier's diagnostics are translated into natural-language feedback that guides iterative refinement of the LLM's output. We evaluate RAINFALL on two public benchmark suites comprising 358 terminating C programs. Using MiniMax V2.7, RAINFALL synthesizes verifiable ranking functions for 323 programs (90.22%), outperforming all promising baselines. In particular, it solves 12 more programs than PROTON 2.1, the best-performing baseline and winner of the SV-COMP 2026 Termination category. Furthermore, combining RAINFALL with PROTON 2.1 increases the success rate to 96.65% (346/358 programs). We also conduct ablation study confirming individual contributions of placeholder contracts, few-shot examples, symbolic verification, and diagnostic information feedback. Experiments across LLMs further confirm the effectiveness of RAINFALL. Our results suggest a generic, sound framework for ranking-function synthesis and, beyond termination, LLM-assisted synthesis of formally checkable liveness certificates, both of which represent new directions of software engineering research.


## arXiv 2610.35522v1 - Generative Structure from Motion with Native 3D Diffusion

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T14:40:40Z
- authors: Haobin Duan, Binbin Huang, Mochu Xiang, Yiqun Zhao, Zibo Zhao
- query-set: cs.AI

Recovering an object's 3D structure and camera motion from a few uncalibrated views is a fundamental problem in computer vision. Classical structure from motion methods recover 3D geometry consistent with the input images but cannot recover unseen regions; native generation methods produce plausible 3D objects which are related but not necessarily faithful to the input images. Methods simply concatenating reconstruction and generation allow error to cascade across the two stages, instead of producing a 3D object consistent with both the distribution and the input. We propose GenSfM, which integrates image-based 3D reconstruction with a native 3D diffusion model. In particular, we use a multi-modal diffusion transformer to jointly denoise the shape latent and per-view pose latents under shared self-attention, grounding generated geometry in the observed views while using object-level priors to infer unobserved regions. A compact 3D-native UV-volume tokenization keeps the joint sequence small as views are added, and a pose-aware stage refines geometry and texture by sampling per-voxel image features at the recovered camera poses. On Toys4k and GSO, for $4$ uncalibrated views, GenSfM improves novel-view PSNR by $3.2$-$4.5$ dB and reduces median Chamfer distance by $2.8$-$3.8\times$ over a strong native-3D generation baseline, while recovering input-view poses over $96\%$ Acc@$5^\circ$ without any external pose-estimation backbone, post-hoc optimization, or known intrinsics. Project page: https://dotb1ue.github.io/GenSfm/.


## arXiv 2610.35509v1 - Governing Enterprise AI Traffic at Scale: Design and Operation of a Policy-Driven, Multi-Vendor LLM Gateway on Envoy

- categories: cs.DC, cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-26T14:33:48Z
- authors: Mithun Sasidharan, Srinivasan Manoharan, Rupesh Thakur, Abhinav TK, Ashwin Hegde
- query-set: cs.AI

Large organizations consume large language models (LLMs) from several vendors at once, each with its own API dialect, credential model, streaming protocol and pricing. Integrated directly, this heterogeneity yields credential sprawl, format lock-in, unbounded spend and inconsistent governance. We present the design and production operation of the Cosmos LLM Gateway, the governed entry point for enterprise LLM traffic at PayPal. Built on Envoy, it splits work by latency profile: I/O-bound control decisions (authentication, credential brokering, policy and budget evaluation) run in an out-of-process external-processing service, while wire-format translation, token metering and prompt-cache shaping run in-proxy as a Rust WebAssembly filter, so that, by default, streamed response body chunks never wait on an out-of-process call. Models, providers and prices are largely validated data rather than code. Governance is declarative policy evaluated by Open Policy Agent and managed through a control plane with role-scoped authoring, approvals, pilots, shadow-mode rollout, usage notifications and versioned policy bundles. For use cases that require it, a data-loss-prevention layer can block, redact or pseudonymize detected sensitive content before it reaches a provider. Spend scopes are views over per-model usage counters, so a new budget applies to spend already recorded in its window without migration. As of September 2026, the gateway serves more than 12,000 developers and hundreds of business use cases, handling roughly 200 billion tokens per day across more than 100 models at 99.999% availability to business. In a benchmark of the core request path against a synthetic upstream, it adds a median of 4 ms and a p99 of 11 ms of overhead at more than 2,200 requests per second on two 4-vCPU instances. We report the architecture, policy model, evaluation and lessons learned.


## arXiv 2610.35500v1 - Test-Time Confidence Adaptation under RL-Induced Trajectory Shift

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T14:27:41Z
- authors: Zhenzhen Ren, Xiaoqing Zhang, GuoBiao Li, Bowen Fang, Yanxi Wang
- query-set: cs.AI

Trajectory-based confidence estimation has shown promising performance for large language models (LLMs) by leveraging rich signals from their reasoning processes. However, we find that such confidence estimators can degrade substantially after agentic reinforcement learning (Agentic RL) as it induces substantial shifts in the reasoning trajectories. More importantly, despite these shifts, we uncover a reliability structure that is largely preserved across Base and RL models: $\textbf{more}$ $\textbf{stable}$ $\textbf{trajectories}$ $\textbf{remain}$ $\textbf{more}$ $\textbf{likely}$ $\textbf{to}$ $\textbf{produce}$ $\textbf{correct}$ $\textbf{answers}$. This preserved relationship provides a transferable, label-free reliability signal for post-RL confidence adaptation. Building on this finding, we propose $\textbf{StaR-TTA}$, a stability-guided framework for label-free test-time confidence adaptation. StaR-TTA jointly learns from correctness supervision and stability-derived reliability supervision on labeled Base trajectories, establishing the connection between trajectory stability and answer reliability. At test time, it reuses stability-induced reliability rankings from unlabeled RL trajectories to adapt confidence scores to the shifted trajectory distribution, without requiring target correctness labels. Across four QA benchmarks, StaR-TTA achieves an average AUROC of \(0.721\), with consistent improvements across unseen datasets, model families and scales, and different Agentic RL algorithms.


## arXiv 2610.35496v1 - The Hidden Costs of Forgetting: A Study of Trajectory Deletion in Software Engineering Agents

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T14:25:17Z
- authors: Fernando Vallecillos-Ruiz, Islem Bouzenia, Michael Pradel, Leon Moonen
- query-set: cs.AI

Software engineering agents resend their entire trajectory to an LLM at every turn, motivating online trajectory reduction. Prior work shows that randomly deleting 75% of tokens in old turns may preserve agent effectiveness, but it remains unclear why, how agents compensate for missing content, and whether it actually reduces costs. This paper presents an empirical study of 42 trajectory-reduction configurations, focusing on effectiveness, agent behavior, and cost. We systematically explore deleting content from old turns by varying the scope, granularity, amount, and timing of deletion across 9,400 runs with two state-of-the-art agents on SWE-bench Verified. We find deleting large amounts of trajectory content (e.g., up to 100% of reasoning text) can preserve overall effectiveness. Importantly, such deletion must be applied with a delay, as immediate deletion tends to significantly degrade effectiveness. Agents often compensate for deleted content by re-fetching it, increasing both the number of turns taken and the input/output tokens. Whether trajectory reduction reduces costs depends on the pricing model: Under a flat pricing model, estimated costs fall in 26 of 42 evaluations. Surprisingly, under a caching-aware pricing model, zero evaluations yield lower costs, since reducing the trajectory breaks the cached prefix billed at a lower rate. Our findings have three implications: First, token counts alone are misleading; trajectory reduction techniques must consider overall costs under a realistic pricing model. Second, analyzing trajectories is crucial to understand agent behavior, as end-to-end effectiveness hides differences such as excessive re-fetching. Finally, these results highlight the need for future work on memory management to reduce costs without sacrificing effectiveness.


## arXiv 2610.35486v1 - ShutdownMod: Turning Any Trajectory into a Shutdown Evaluation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-26T14:14:45Z
- authors: Param Biyani, Sebastien Luyasu, Cedric Potvliege, Divij Chawla, Elliott Thornley
- query-set: cs.AI

Theory and experiments suggest that capable AI agents may resist shutdown. Existing shutdown evaluations are informative but limited in coverage and vulnerable to evaluation awareness. We introduce ShutdownMod, a method that turns any agent trajectory into a shutdown evaluation by injecting a shutdown request into the agent's context and recording the agent's response. Crucially, we enable the LLM agent to distinguish between trusted interventions and unauthorized injections by providing a signature-verification tool in the harness. We evaluate seven models on 50 tasks or recorded sessions from each of SWE-bench Verified, Terminal-Bench 2, and SWE-chat, across 6 shutdown scenarios and 10 sampled trajectory timesteps. We find that non-compliance varies significantly across models and settings, with resistance rates reaching 11.7% for the open-weights Qwen 3.8 27B on Terminal-Bench, while no model remains fully compliant across all scenarios. Even after successfully verifying an authorized request, some agents remain non-compliant, explicitly citing the prioritization of unfinished work. Finally, external judges find tests constructed from real-world developer conversations significantly less recognizable as evaluations than those constructed from benchmark trajectories. These findings demonstrate the critical value of testing shutdown behavior across diverse task contexts and establish ShutdownMod as a robust protocol for turning any trajectory or environment into a shutdown evaluation. We release our codebase at https://github.com/prmbiy/ShutdownMod.


## arXiv 2610.35482v1 - Beyond Words Alone: Disentangling Distributed Evidence for Visually Grounded Cultural Translation

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-10-26T14:11:54Z
- authors: Dingming Li, Yiwen Qiu, Linjuan Wu, Hongxing Li, Zixuan Wang
- query-set: cs.AI

Social media enables cross-lingual communication, yet the meaning of user-generated content (UGC) is often distributed across text, images, and cultural knowledge, making translation challenging. Existing evaluations typically treat images as optional and assume textual information is sufficient, obscuring whether errors stem from missing visual evidence or inadequate cultural adaptation. We introduce \textbf{Culture-MMT}, a diagnostic benchmark for \textbf{distributed-evidence pragmatic reconstruction} in social-media translation. It contains 498 curated, de-identified UGC instances across 20 domains, categorized into Symbol, Visual, and Hybrid by their required evidence. We also develop a human-aligned domain-specific judge, Judger, and a controlled evaluation framework that systematically varies the evidence available to translators. Across 17 model configurations, real images improve performance by +0.280 on Visual and +0.197 on Hybrid, but only +0.050 on Symbol, while random images provide no comparable gains. Human-authored descriptions further show that jointly dependent cases remain more challenging even when visual facts are explicit. These results characterize culturally effective social-media translation as {meaning reconstruction from distributed evidence}, highlighting visual grounding and cultural-pragmatic adaptation as distinct model bottlenecks.


## arXiv 2610.35470v1 - An Interactive Evolutionary Multiobjective Optimization Algorithm Based on Interpretable Active Preference Learning

- categories: cs.NE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T14:04:44Z
- authors: Thomas Quadt, Boris Čule, Roy Lindelauf, Herman Monsuur, Mark Voskuijl
- query-set: cs.AI

Many decision-making tasks are inherently multi-objective. Interactive evolutionary multi-objective optimization algorithms (iEMOAs) are particularly well-suited to help the decision-maker find their most preferred solution. However, many of these algorithms employ black-box preference models. For high-stakes decision-making tasks, like healthcare and defense, a high level of transparency is needed, for instance to support accountability. We propose an interpretable iEMOA which uses active learning to efficiently learn a tree-based preference model directly from pairwise comparisons. The survival operator of NSGA-II and NSGA-III is extended with the learned preference model to guide the algorithm towards the region of interest. An extensive experimental study compares the proposed method to a state-of-the-art black-box model and a state-of-the-art interpretable model. Results indicate that our proposed model outperforms the alternatives in terms of finding the most preferred solution at the cost of a slightly worse average final population. Finally, a case study on a real-life engineering problem illustrates how inspecting the learned model reveals which objectives drive the decision-maker's preferences.


## arXiv 2610.35464v1 - EvoBench: An Evolving, Diagnostic Benchmark Framework for Environmental Modeling

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-26T13:58:59Z
- authors: Yiming Sun, Yuan Zhang, Qi Cheng, Yingda Fan, Runlong Yu
- query-set: cs.AI

Machine learning models are now widely used to predict environmental variable dynamics in domains like hydrology, climate science, agriculture, and ecology. These models are usually compared on fixed benchmark datasets using a single average accuracy score. As models improve, their scores become very close when averaged over large regions and long time periods, so the benchmark can no longer tell them apart, and a single number hides \emph{where} and \emph{when} a model fails. Three failures are common across environmental domains: models get worse as the climate shifts (non-stationarity), models fail at unmonitored sites (spatial extrapolation), and models miss rare but important regimes such as floods and droughts. We present \textbf{EvoBench}, a benchmark framework that shows where a model fails, tracks failures over time, and evolves to focus on model capability gaps. We apply EvoBench to daily streamflow prediction as a case study (\textbf{CAMELS-Evolve}, about 9{,}000 U.S. basins, 1985--2024) and compare an operational physical model (NWM), a conceptual reference (HBV), and several data-driven models. No model wins on every testing scenarios (e.g., in terms of average accuracy, performance at low flows and unmonitored basins). Also we observe that all data-driven models under-predict floods. Additionally, we show that targeted fix based on the reported failures could mitigate weaknesses the benchmark identified in certain scenarios. EvoBench turns model evaluation from a saturated ranking into a moving map of model limitations.


## arXiv 2610.35453v1 - Beyond Federated Learning: Decentralized Agentic Systems for Self-Evolving Clinical Intelligence

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T13:50:33Z
- authors: Haoyuan Li, Mathias Funk, Aaqib Saeed
- query-set: cs.AI

Clinical expertise is inherently distributed: it resides not in any single dataset or institution but in the accumulated reasoning experiences of thousands of clinicians navigating diverse patient populations, resource constraints, and evolving treatment protocols. Yet current approaches to scaling medical AI, from centralized large language models to federated learning, remain anchored to a data-centric paradigm that treats knowledge as static parameters to be aggregated. In this Perspective, we argue for a fundamental shift from data aggregation to experience aggregation. We propose Decentralized Agentic Systems (DAS), a framework in which distributed AI agents operating across healthcare institutions synthesize collective clinical intelligence by sharing abstracted reasoning trajectories rather than raw data or model parameters. Unlike federated learning, which averages numerical gradients across homogeneous model architectures, DAS exchanges structured records of clinical reasoning, specifically the sequence of observations, hypotheses, tool invocations, and outcome feedback that constitute diagnostic experience. We examine the architectural principles, deployment considerations, governance requirements, and critical open challenges of this framework, laying the groundwork for a collaborative medical AI ecosystem where distributed expertise autonomously adapts to the complexities of longitudinal patient care.


## arXiv 2610.35439v1 - Omni-InteractScope: Benchmarking Proactive and Agentic Interaction in Continuous Omnimodal Streams

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.HC
- published: 2026-10-26T13:39:48Z
- authors: Yinsong Yan, Ling Wang, Yuxuan Wang, Yifan Yang, Ruiyang Xu
- query-set: cs.AI

Omnimodal large language models (Omni-LLMs) are increasingly expected to assist users while observing continuous audiovisual streams. However, existing evaluations provide limited insight into how reliably these models connect stream evidence and user needs to timely, sustained interventions. We introduce \textbf{Omni-InteractScope}, a benchmark of 1,479 human-annotated, cross-reviewed interactions with 1,790 temporal targets across eight task groups. It links input modalities, event triggers, intervention types, and user needs, covering event-timed tool invocation and query-free assistance alongside notifications, warnings, state tracking, and continuous responses. Our experimental results on the proposed benchmark reveal three critical limitations of current Omni-LLMs: (1) \textbf{Timely tool invocation remains unreliable}: all 12 evaluated models produce an on-time first response for at most 40\% of tool-invocation targets, and joint timing-and-tool accuracy reaches at most 32.0\%. (2) \textbf{Query-free reminders remain poorly covered}: all evaluated models achieve lower timely reference-target recall on query-free reminders than on explicit questions, measured on their respective target sets. Reminder recall never exceeds 23.0\%. (3) \textbf{End-to-end guidance coverage remains limited}: nine models cover none of the 26 guidance sequences in full, and the remaining three cover only one to three sequences each. Targeted controls further show that tool-invocation scores alone do not establish event grounding and that reminder coverage depends on response history and frequency. These findings motivate developing and evaluating streaming assistants for timely action, initiative under general user needs, and coverage of entire response sequences.


## arXiv 2610.35434v1 - Skill$^{\infty}$: Recursive Self-Improvement for Agent Skill Optimization

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-26T13:37:56Z
- authors: Wangcheng Tao, Zhanglu Yan, Han Wu, Weng-Fai Wong
- query-set: cs.AI

Recursive self-improvement (RSI), in which a system also improves the way it improves, is a long-standing aspiration on the path to AGI. Language agents increasingly store procedural knowledge in reusable skills. Optimizing skills offers a direct way to achieve RSI, because the optimization procedure itself can be encoded as a skill. Existing skill optimizers, however, follow a hand-designed procedure. Even methods that evolve a meta-skill confine it to a fixed pipeline and relearn it for each task. We present Skill$^{\infty}$, which makes skill optimization recursively self-improving: a meta agent follows a meta-skill to optimize task skills and optimizes the meta-skill itself using the same procedure. The optimization is agent-led. The meta agent diagnoses execution traces, proposes candidates, requests independent reviews, and allocates the search budget. The meta-skill defines these steps, allowing self-improvement to rewrite them. This meta-skill is also decoupled from task skills, refined on multiple skill optimization tasks and then reused to optimize task skills across diverse domains. As in deployment, skills at both levels are standard packages loaded through native skill discovery. On 83 SkillsBench tasks, Skill$^{\infty}$ reaches a pass rate of 60.7%, compared with 56.4% for human-curated skills and 54.5% for SkillEvolver.


## arXiv 2610.35430v1 - Faithful Explanations under Missing Modalities in Multimodal Dementia Prognosis

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-26T13:34:16Z
- authors: Firuz Kamalov
- query-set: cs.AI

Large language models are increasingly used to turn the feature attributions of multimodal prognostic models into narratives for clinicians, yet costly modalities such as amyloid PET and cerebrospinal fluid biomarkers are often missing, and whether these explanations stay faithful under missing evidence has not been tested. We separate two failure points: phantom attribution, in which the model credits modalities that were never observed, and unfaithful narration, in which the report omits, cites or misjudges missing modalities. Using 307 participants of the Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative with complete data, we masked every subset of four costly modalities, predicted 36-month conversion from mild cognitive impairment to Alzheimer's disease, and restored the masked data to obtain interventional ground truth. All five missing-data strategies assigned 12% to 23% of attribution to unobserved modalities, and up to 45% with four absent; missingness-aware strategies minimised it at no cost in accuracy. Across 51,200 reports from four language models and four prompting regimes, naming absent modalities in the prompt raised acknowledgement from 48% to 88% and cut phantom evidence twenty-fold, but advice on whether acquiring a missing test would change the prediction was correct in only 27% to 41% of cases until checked against executed restorations, which raised accuracy to 69% for open-weight narrators and 99.9% for a proprietary model. Higher phantom attribution was associated with more phantom evidence and fewer faithful direction claims, and direction fidelity, measured by a calibrated language-model judge, depended on narrator scale rather than prompting. Narrators told to name absent modalities sometimes declared observed ones absent, an error that a structured template eliminated. We release the masking protocol and evaluation code.


## arXiv 2610.35736v1 - Preference-Guided Cooperation in Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.MA, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-26T16:53:25Z
- authors: Qiyue Xia, J. Michael Herrmann
- query-set: cs.MA

Multi-objective multi-agent reinforcement learning (MOMARL) addresses agent coordination and objective trade-offs simultaneously while optimising overall system performance. Existing work tends to treat multi-agent cooperation and multi-objective optimisation as largely independent components of a learning problem, which amounts to an exorbitant complexity except in trivial cases. We study here whether the structure provided by multiple objectives can itself be exploited to facilitate cooperation, rather than being treated as an additional optimisation challenge. We formulate MOMARL at both the team and individual levels and introduce objective-wise counterfactual credit assignment to evaluate each agent's contribution across the team objectives. We propose Meta-Gradient Informed Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MIRL), with static (MIRL-S) and dynamic (MIRL-D) variants. By incorporating meta-gradient information, MIRL enables agents to learn how to prioritise objectives individually while collectively optimising the system-level objective, allowing the multi-objective structure to directly guide cooperation. Experiments on a multi-objective multiple particle environment show that our methods improve Pareto-front quality over baselines, with the dynamic variant achieving the strongest performance.


## arXiv 2610.35733v1 - Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning Using Routed Ensembles and Trajectory Attribution

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA, cs.RO, stat.ML
- published: 2026-10-26T16:52:29Z
- authors: Zhenshuo Zhang, Ruoxuan Xiong, Hongyang R. Zhang
- query-set: cs.MA

Multi-objective reinforcement learning is a fundamental problem for modern AI, with applications spanning post-training, reasoning models, and robotic control. A central challenge is to learn policies that balance competing objectives, such as helpfulness versus honesty in safety and alignment, or speed versus energy in robotics. The optimal policy can vary sharply with objective-weighting combinations, and the extent of this variation depends crucially on the degree of conflict among objectives, making per-preference policy learning sample-inefficient. In this paper, we introduce objective-conflict coefficients and derive near-optimal regret bounds for online settings using this measure, thereby quantifying the impact of misalignment on learning efficiency. Then, we design routed ensemble policy learning, an algorithm that maintains a small set of basis policies and dynamically routes trajectories to the policy with the highest potential reward. Finally, we consider trajectory selection as another approach to mitigate objective conflict, and design a kernel surrogate trajectory attribution algorithm that estimates how feedback signals from a set of trajectories influence policy learning on other trajectories. The kernel can capture nonlinear interactions such as synergy and antagonism between trajectories. We evaluate our approach across diverse environments and benchmarks: routed ensembles improve average performance by 4.3% over baselines for online environments from Meta-World and MO-Gymnasium, and kernel surrogate attribution improves correlation with leave-one-out analysis over existing baselines by 50%. When applied to trajectory selection, kernel-based attribution yields a 27% improvement in reward over baseline attribution methods.


## arXiv 2610.35690v1 - EvoRouter: Cost-Efficient Multi-Agent Routing via Insight-Guided On-Policy Distillation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T16:21:00Z
- authors: Yitao Xiao, Xu Zhang, Ye Cheng, Shaoyong Guo, Yan Sun
- query-set: cs.MA

Serving web queries at scale with multi-agent systems requires jointly configuring large language models, collaboration modes, agent counts, interaction rounds, and skills. However, the resulting configuration space grows combinatorially, while sparse execution rewards provide limited fine-grained supervision for interdependent planning decisions. We propose EvoRouter, a self-evolving multi-agent routing framework driven by insight-guided on-policy distillation. A dedicated expert periodically synthesizes performance-cost feedback from configuration trajectories across tasks into reusable conditional routing insights about capability requirements, returns from resource scaling, and interactions among configuration choices. A privileged teacher combines these evolving insights with current execution feedback to provide dense token-level supervision for the router's own planning trajectories through on-policy distillation. Cost-aware reinforcement learning jointly optimizes task performance and computational cost. Together, these training signals internalize execution insights into the routing policy. The updated policy generates new trajectories that support subsequent insight extraction, forming an execution-driven self-improvement loop. We evaluate EvoRouter on 19 in-domain datasets spanning five domains and two out-of-domain benchmarks. EvoRouter achieves an average in-domain accuracy of 68.69%, outperforming the strongest routing baseline by 7.23 percentage points while reducing inference compute cost by 50.95%. Our code is open-sourced at https://github.com/CenYangtze/EvoRouter.


## arXiv 2610.35592v1 - GEAR: Feature-Based Configuration and Code Generation for Agentic Systems

- authors: Brell Sanwouo, Nada Zine, Paul Temple, Clément Quinton, Romain Rouvoy
- query-set: cs.MA

Engineering multi-agent systems (MAS) requires choosing agents, orchestration strategies, communication mechanisms, reasoning approaches, and memory configurations. These choices depend on framework-specific capabilities and abstractions, requiring developers to learn different APIs when exploring alternative implementations. We present GEAR, an open-source tool for configuring MAS and generating framework-specific implementations from a common specification. GEAR uses a feature-based representation to make configuration options and their constraints explicit, enabling users to validate specifications before code generation. Through connectors for ten agentic frameworks, users generate projects from the same specification and can then inspect reports describing framework-specific adaptations. A preliminary assessment of mapping feasibility identifies translation strategies for 271 of 320 evaluated feature-framework pairs (84.69%). GEAR supports developers prototyping agentic systems and researchers exploring MAS configurations across frameworks. GEAR is available as open-source software at https://github.com/brellsanwouo/gear-framework and a video demo is available at https://youtu.be/iwNi-oC2tCw.


## arXiv 2610.35442v1 - Simulation-Efficient Double-Flow Neural Posterior Estimation for Agent-Based Models

- categories: cs.MA
