- authors: Junyao Zhang, Jinglai Li, Junqi Tang
- query-set: cs.MA

Neural posterior estimation (NPE) enables Bayesian inference of the posterior parameter in agent-based models (ABMs) with intractable likelihoods, but simulating training data can be computationally expensive. We propose Double-Flow Neural Posterior Estimation (DF-NPE), which combines learned time-series summaries with two conditional normalizing flows for synthetic-data generation and posterior estimation. The framework supplements direct simulations with synthetic training pairs and supports both pooled and ordered training strategies. Experiments in two settings of the Brock-Hommes asset-pricing model show that DF-NPE can reduce direct simulations by 90 percent while achieving lower mean posterior discrepancies and runtime than standard NPE. Performance varies with the allocation of directly simulated and synthetic data and the training strategy. These results establish learned summary augmentation as a practical approach to reducing simulation costs for Bayesian posterior inference of parameters in ABMs.


## arXiv 2610.35398v1 - Belief-Conditioned Multi-Agent Actor Critic for Decentralized LLM Collaboration

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T13:12:41Z
- authors: Tianle Chen, Shuo Liu, Christopher Amato
- query-set: cs.MA

Recent work has explored optimizing LLM collaboration through Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL), but many approaches still rely on predefined workflows, limiting efficiency and scalability. Decentralized LLM collaboration is challenging because agents execute independently with limited communication, share a joint objective, and must infer useful information from communication traces. In this paper, we propose BCMAAC, a Belief-Conditioned Multi-Agent Actor-Critic method that maintains beliefs over contextual information and uses them to facilitate training. Experiments on Minecraft game-playing, web searching and tool-using tasks show that BCMAAC improves task performance and collaborative behavior compared to naive actor-critic baselines. The improvements are particularly pronounced in multi-turn tasks with richer contextual information, where belief modeling provides a clear advantage.


## arXiv 2610.35335v1 - Knowing When to Escalate: Disagreement Patterns for Uncertainty-Aware Content Moderation

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T12:32:25Z
- authors: Michał Wawer, Jarosław A. Chudziak
- query-set: cs.MA

Agentic content-moderation systems increasingly rely on collective judgments from LLM agents, while aggregation methods commonly treat inter-agent disagreement as a reliability problem to be removed through consensus or voting. This framing is inappropriate for value-laden tasks, where disagreement may reflect irreducible value pluralism - a distinction standard uncertainty decompositions cannot recover, since they operate on output distributions and discard the reasoning behind each judgment. We propose Reasoning Trace Divergence Analysis (RTDA), which embeds model-generated justifications from perspective-differentiated agents and classifies each case by justification similarity and conclusion agreement. The resulting four-state taxonomy isolates convergent disagreement, where agents reasoning similarly but concluding differently, as a candidate signature of value-laden conflict. On 1,000 items from the Measuring Hate Speech corpus, this structural signal predicts human annotator disagreement better than magnitude-only and output-only baselines. We contribute a design principle: condition human escalation on the structure of disagreement, not its magnitude alone, with RTDA serving as a post-processing layer for systems that already generate reasoning traces.


## arXiv 2610.34968v1 - Guarded Agent-in-the-Loop Residual Adaptation for Evolving Wind Power Regimes

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T07:38:33Z
- authors: Yuhang Dong, Min Xia, Jinghong Li, Yue Dai, Xinyue Wang
- query-set: cs.MA

Wind power forecasting models are often deployed as fixed temporal backbones, but the errors they make can vary across meteorological regimes such as cold waves, heat-and-calm periods, productive typhoon, and destructive storms. We present a guarded Agent-in-the-loop residual adaptation framework that leaves the temporal backbone unchanged and adapts only a small set of scene-conditioned residual experts. An Agent layer interprets external scene information, identifies the current regime, selects residual experts, and applies a correction only when recent observed errors support it. New experts enter the pool after they outperform their initializations on a validation set, keeping model growth under explicit control. Backbone-only and Agent-enhanced forecasts are compared at lead times of 4, 8, 12, 24, and 48 across nine time-series backbones. Across these runs, the guarded framework lowers both MAE and RMSE for all 45 backbone-horizon combinations, with average MAE reductions ranging from 11.21% to 20.26% across backbones. Online-selection baselines, component ablations, and routing analyses identify the roles of residual experts, scene-aware routing, and the online acceptance rule. Candidate-scene runs evaluate the promotion path under controlled conditions; long-term unattended deployment was not evaluated.


## arXiv 2610.34878v1 - ClinVerse: A Realistic Multi-Agent Diagnostic Environment for Training and Evaluating Medical Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T05:50:57Z
- authors: Sirry Chen, Fangke Chen, Xuanwen Ding, Wei Chen, Zhongyu Wei
- query-set: cs.MA

Disease diagnosis is a key application of large language model (LLM) agents in healthcare. However, existing medical agents are primarily trained and evaluated with static question-answering data, providing limited opportunities to learn active information acquisition, examination decision-making, and dynamic clinical interaction. To address this limitation, we present ClinVerse, an interactive, real-data-grounded multi-agent environment designed for training and evaluating medical diagnostic agents. Built on ClinPatient, a scalable dataset comprising 150k diagnostic cases derived from real-world electronic health records, ClinVerse supports coordinated interactions among six specialized clinical roles. Specifically, a doctor agent starts from an initial patient complaint, progressively gathers clinical evidence through patient interviews and examination requests, and ultimately produces a diagnosis. For each interaction trajectory, a rule-based scoring framework quantifies information acquisition, examination ordering, and diagnostic accuracy. These trajectory-level scores serve as reward signals for agent training while also enabling scalable evaluation. In experiments, we evaluate various general-purpose and medical-domain models and show that nine self-evolution methods across three categories consistently improve agents' diagnostic capabilities within ClinVerse. We further analyze the interplay among model capability, environment feedback, and self-evolution methods.


## arXiv 2610.34836v1 - Collective Emergent Alignment: Measuring and Amplifying Interaction-Induced Reasoning Alignment in Multi-Agent LLMs

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T04:31:56Z
- authors: Boxuan Wang, Zhuoyun Li, Sarvapali D. Ramchurn, Xiaowei Huang, Yi Dong
- query-set: cs.MA

Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems can exhibit emergent behaviors through interaction, yet whether a similar phenomenon exists for reasoning alignment remains largely unexplored. We study whether agent interaction produces additional reference-grounded reasoning alignment beyond matched individual self-refinement, and introduce REMAP (Reference-Entailment-Matrix for Agentic Process), a framework that quantifies this effect through Semantic Coverage and interaction-specific alignment gains. Across diverse model families and two multi-hop reasoning benchmarks, we observe broadly positive interaction gains, particularly on reasoning content that depends on integrating evidence distributed across agents. These gains are consistently associated with independently measured peer-information recovery, while further analysis reveals composition-dependent heterogeneity effects and asymmetric information transfer across agent capability levels. Motivated by these findings, we further introduce a fact-level multi-agent communication framework that replaces full reasoning-trace exchange with compact, gap-aware, and selectively routed information. Our experiments show that compact fact exchange improves reasoning alignment and task performance while reducing communication overhead, whereas more selective communication exposes a trade-off between acquiring distributed information, retaining local content, and communication efficiency. Overall, our results show that collective emergent alignment can be quantified, linked to information-transfer patterns, and used to guide more effective multi-agent communication.


## arXiv 2610.34644v1 - SlowLab: Benchmarking Autonomous AI Agents on a Year of Experiments They Cannot Undo

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-26T00:09:19Z
- authors: Ningpei Ding, Zai Wen Feng
- query-set: cs.MA

AI agents are increasingly proposed as autonomous experimenters, yet the benchmarks that test them mostly offer cheap experiments that answer at once. Experiments in physical facilities are different: they take months, occupy scarce equipment and cannot be undone. We present SlowLab, a benchmark in which an agent runs a one-year campaign in four simulated greenhouse compartments on a site whose crop, facility and prices are hidden. The agent decides when to plant which climate policy, whether to stop a crop and which measurements to read while crops grow, and at the end recommends one policy, which a private evaluator scores in weather the agent never saw. In a preregistered study on 48 unseen sites, three low-cost language-model agents ended near a standard grower setting and clearly below a Bayesian-optimisation baseline that starts from that setting. They ran the facility as two synchronous batches, so measurements taken during the season never changed what they planted, and these measurements improved no method's result. Letting other methods re-read the evidence a campaign had collected did not yield significantly better recommendations, which points to where the models start and which experiments they try. Code is released under the MIT license.


## arXiv 2610.34634v1 - AgentProofBench: Benchmarking Reusable Verifiers for Web Agents

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-25T23:20:28Z
- authors: Jingjie Ning, Xueqi Li, Yi Lu, Yibo Kong
- query-set: cs.MA

Reusable Web-agent verification requires a rule to follow changing requirements and support subsequent execution. We introduce AgentProofBench, which connects paired tests of a frozen acceptance rule to execution from identical saved openings. The resource spans 97 tasks, 79 families, and four Web services. Experiments with DeepSeek Flash/Pro establish two linked measurements. Across 48 families, complete-panel and decision-replay tests retain 82/96 Flash and 79/96 Pro source programs; paired reuse-rebuild BA intervals are [-5.47, 1.57] and [-10.16, 0.00] percentage points. Across 22 execution families, deployment-state panel correctness separates program assignments whose two saved openings are both delivered correctly: 32/33 assignments with error-free panels, versus 1/11 with panel errors. All reference-defined program-gate regressions and withheld deliveries occur in the latter group. Neutral continuation attains the highest correct delivery under the tested generic feedback, providing a concrete baseline for measuring the value added by a gate. Released instructions, states, programs, reference rules, and trajectories connect each test failure to its execution consequences. AgentProofBench makes reusable verification assessable at both the requirement and policy levels.


## arXiv 2610.34625v1 - Online Fair Division with Frequency Predictions

- categories: cs.GT, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-25T22:48:13Z
- authors: Tzeh Yuan Neoh, Nicholas Teh
- query-set: cs.MA

We study online fair division of indivisible goods among $n$ agents with frequency predictions: before any good arrives, the algorithm learns the multiset of each agent's values for the goods, but neither the arrival order nor which values of different agents belong to the same good. Neoh et al. (ICML 2026) showed that such predictions allow every share-based guarantee that is achievable offline, and left open whether a positive approximation of envy-freeness up to one good (EF1) is possible. We show that deterministic online algorithms can guarantee $1/2$-EF1 for three agents and $(3-\sqrt5)/2$-EF1 for four agents, whereas for five or more agents, no online algorithm can guarantee any positive approximation of EF1, even with $n+1$ goods and identical predictions. Together with the known result for two agents, a positive approximation of EF1 is therefore possible if and only if $n\leq4$. We also rule out any positive approximation of EFX for three or more agents, even when the valuations take only two distinct positive values. Next, we use the meta-algorithm of Neoh et al. (ICML 2026) to compute an epistemic EFX (EEFX) allocation in polynomial time for any $n$, and hence a $1/(n-1)$-EF$(n-2)$ allocation for $n\geq3$, whereas algorithms that fix a picking sequence in advance can guarantee a positive approximation of EF$k$ if and only if $n\leq k+2$. Finally, for noisy predictions, we give algorithms that return EEFX allocations when the predictions are exact, and guarantee $(1-2\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^2))$-EEFX under underestimates and, for $n\geq3$, $(1-2(n-1)\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^2))$-EEFX under overestimates; the linear terms are the best possible, and larger overestimates rule out any positive approximation for such algorithms.


## arXiv 2610.34569v1 - Agentic AI Orchestration Framework for Spatial Intelligence: Supply Chain Deployment

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA, cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-25T21:12:21Z
- authors: Constantinos Papantoniou, Brian Hilton
- query-set: cs.MA

Industrial spatial decisions are assembled across systems that share neither a reference frame nor an owner, and analysts spend substantial effort reconciling those systems rather than reasoning about space. This paper presents ANASSA, a reference architecture integrating structured reasoning, multi-agent workflow orchestration, authoritative spatial grounding, and feedback-driven execution, and reports its instantiation in an operating supply-chain deployment. The architecture places typed data contracts at agent boundaries, validates spatial claims against a certified authoritative foundation, and enforces two blocking human-in-the-loop approvals inside a six-step cognitive loop. The deployment covers multi-hazard risk assessment on an ocean-and-inland freight lane, including cold-chain excursion detection over container telemetry. Across a three-month proof of concept (POC) with three implementation iterations, three deployment outcomes are reported: 25% improvement in data quality, 50% reduction in spatial workflow runtime, and 60% lower decision latency relative to the preceding manual workflow. Absolute deployment-scale quantities and baseline counts are confidential; reported percentages are observed relative changes. These are uncontrolled deployment observations, not controlled experiments. We therefore specify instrumentation and ablation protocols for mechanism-level evaluation.


## arXiv 2610.34564v1 - FlockWorld: Toward Population-Scale Multi-Agent Video World Models through Artificial Life Simulation

- categories: cs.CV, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-25T21:07:26Z
- authors: Meryem Koksal, Yunzhe Wang, Volkan Ustun
- query-set: cs.MA

Most action-conditioned video world models remain limited to a single player, while existing multiplayer models support only small, fixed groups of agents. We introduce FlockWorld, an artificial-life environment built on Reynolds's Boids algorithm for studying population-scale multi-agent video world models. Specifically, FlockWorld renders synchronized egocentric crops, assigns distinct colors to preserve camera-agent identity across views, exposes rule-derived accelerations as actions, and lets the number of camera agents be configured independently of the size of the surrounding population. We further introduce FlockDiT, an action-conditioned latent diffusion transformer trained with flow matching on FlockWorld data to jointly generate synchronized egocentric videos for multiple agents. Our results suggest that joint generation benefits agent detection at density-matched settings and that diffusion forcing improves rollout fidelity. Together, FlockWorld and FlockDiT provide a controlled testbed for multi-agent video generation.


## arXiv 2610.34467v1 - DynaFlow: Learning Free-Form Multi-Agent Orchestration

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-25T17:38:55Z
- authors: Ruhao Liu, Qi Li, Shizun Wang, Xinchao Wang
- query-set: cs.MA

Multi-agent systems often rely on predefined collaboration structures, limiting their ability to adapt to diverse task requirements. We introduce DynaFlow, a framework that integrates free-form executable workflow synthesis into the action space of the main agent. The agent decides when collaboration is needed and generates task-specific programs that coordinate sub-agents through sequential or parallel execution, structured information exchange, and verification. Execution results and traces inform subsequent actions, enabling the agent to adapt collaboration as reasoning progresses. We train only the main agent through supervised fine-tuning on distilled trajectories, followed by refinement on examples filtered for unsupported premises, biased verification, and information-flow defects. All sub-agents remain fixed, separating orchestration learning from executor capability improvement. Across six benchmarks spanning mathematical reasoning, scientific question answering, and multi-hop retrieval, DynaFlow achieves 88.08% overall accuracy in Forced mode, outperforming the strongest evaluated baseline by 10.90 percentage points with the same Qwen3.5-27B backbone. Workflow analysis finds more explicit use, verification, and refinement of upstream outputs after training, highlighting cross-stage information flow as a key dimension of multi-agent orchestration.


## arXiv 2610.34450v1 - EvolveMAS: A Unified Self-Evolution Framework for Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-25T17:09:49Z
- authors: Yalun Wu, Qiwei Ma, Bingbing Xu, Dan Zhang, Long Bai
- query-set: cs.MA

Decentralized multi-agent systems (MAS) enable large language model agents to interact without centralized control. Agents need to adapt to other agents at test time and reuse accumulated experience to refine their interaction strategies for future interactions across cooperative, mixed-motive, and adversarial settings. However, existing approaches often develop adaptation mechanisms for particular interaction settings. This raises a central question: how can decentralized MAS support test-time adaptation and reliable experience reuse across interaction settings? We propose EvolveMAS, a unified self-evolution framework that supports experience-driven adaptation within and across episodes. EvolveMAS pairs each main agent with a dedicated MemoryAgent for test-time memory governance, which maintains isolated Structured Memory outside the main agent's reasoning context. EvolveMAS organizes adaptation into two complementary processes: Within-Episode Online Adaptation and Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution. During Within-Episode Online Adaptation, MemoryAgent updates episodic, semantic, and working memory from interaction evidence to inform subsequent actions. During Cross-Episode Knowledge Evolution, the main agent uses memory and episode outcomes to distill reusable interaction knowledge comprising partner profiles and interaction strategies for subsequent episodes. Across five benchmarks and six evaluation configurations spanning the three interaction settings, EvolveMAS achieves the highest normalized average score of 77.2 among the compared methods. Ablations show that test-time memory governance and cross-episode knowledge reuse both improve performance. Interaction traces further illustrate how updated memory and distilled knowledge guide subsequent interactions. These results support experience-driven adaptation in decentralized MAS without updating model parameters.


## arXiv 2610.34357v1 - 6G Reasoning Agent Societies: Composition, Scaling, and Consistency-Time Laws

- categories: cs.NI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-25T15:06:47Z
- authors: Hatim Chergui, Carolina Fernández-Martínez, Mustapha Benjillali, Mehdi Bennis, Merouane Debbah
- query-set: cs.MA

Emerging 6G control planes would compose Large Language Model (LLM) agents that exchange interpreted conclusions, so hallucinations can propagate even over error-free transport. Recent architectures audit each message locally against the sender's observation mask, but isolated audits miss collective behavior. Modeling the agent society as a cellular sheaf, we establish three laws valid on arbitrary topologies. First, the \emph{composition law}: network-wide divergence exceeds the sum of per-agent audit scores by exactly their hidden total correlation. This blind spot masks up to 23\% of joint divergence and, because the declared signal cancels in the cognitive signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), overstates it by $1.14$\,dB purely through topology; the resulting trust inflation preserves valid consensus states but slows convergence. Second, the \emph{scaling law}: reconciliation decouples across metric counters, so the worst-connected dimension sets the pace. Consistency time grows for a society size $n$ as $Ω(n^{2/d})$ on polynomial-growth topologies of degree $d$, and paths breach a $1$\,s near-real-time budget at $n^\ast=13$ agents, whereas expanders ($Θ(1)$) and radius-3 local audits ($O(1)$, $\approx300$\,ms) stay bounded. Third, the \emph{consistency-time law}: the timing error from omitted correlations is one-sided and capped by the worst-measured link. These laws, validated on simulated topologies and deployed LLM societies, give provable worst-case latency ceilings for near-real-time operation.


## arXiv 2610.34282v1 - Weighted Fairness in Multi-User Earth Observation Satellite Scheduling

- categories: cs.GT, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-25T13:25:45Z
- authors: Maxime Lucet, Nawal Benabbou, Aurélie Beynier, Nicolas Maudet, Gauthier Picard
- query-set: cs.MA

As constellations of satellite grow in size, and become more costly, they are increasingly funded by multiple stakeholders who expect access to the constellation's resources that is commensurate with their investment. Such cooperation raises significant challenges for constellation operation, in particular regarding the fairness of the computed schedules among stakeholders. While several existing works deal with fairness in the Earth Observation Satellite Scheduling Problem (EOSSP), most of them assume that users (e.g. space agencies, research labs, countries) have the same weights, i.e. contributed equally to the project and therefore have equal access to the constellation. However, in practice, users' contributions often differ significantly. Hence, in this paper, we study a weighted variant of the EOSSP where users are assigned weights reflecting their respective contributions to the constellation. We propose several solution concepts with varying focus on efficiency and fairness, namely weighted utilitarianism, weighted egalitarianism, weighted leximin, and weighted ordered weighed averaging (WOWA). We formulate the related optimization problems as Mixed-Integer Linear Programs (MILPs), and compare them against two natural heuristics: weighted greedy and weighted round robin. All methods are evaluated on scenarios drawn from the literature, under strict runtime constraints, in order to assess the performance of each approach with respect to both efficiency and fairness metrics.


## arXiv 2610.34121v1 - BE-CTDE: Blockchain-Enabled Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning with Anomaly Detection and Token Economics

- categories: cs.MA, cs.CR, cs.LG, cs.NI
- published: 2026-10-25T07:39:23Z
- authors: Chih-Wei Huang, Hsin-Chieh Lee, Yun-Chen Yu, Ibrahim Althamary, Rongxing Lu
- query-set: cs.MA

Multi-agent reinforcement learning supports cooperative decision-making in Internet of Things systems, but strategic deviations can disrupt coordination and undermine confidence in the training process. This paper proposes Blockchain-Enabled Centralized Training and Decentralized Execution (BE-CTDE), which uses blockchain as an active training-management layer. A behavioral verifier compares executed trajectories with shared-policy recommendations; its flags drive a token mechanism combining slashing, burning, rewards, and compensation. Retained balances determine each agent's contribution to the global policy update, connecting auditable behavior to learning influence. We derive a one-round incentive condition that accounts for action-dependent compensation and the payoff from deviation, and a finite expected exclusion-time bound under explicit conditional detection and accounting assumptions, showing perfect detection is unnecessary. An exact finite-population capacity bound characterizes the limits of the population-relative detector. In multi-agent path finding experiments, BE-CTDE outperforms a state-of-the-art learning-based path planner with substantially higher success rates under attack, even when adversaries form the majority. It also outperforms partially defended baselines in all configurations, with collision rates up to 35% lower than under detection without economic consequences and up to 43% lower than under conventional stake slashing. Together, these results characterize how behavioral verification and token feedback can support accountable cooperative learning.


## arXiv 2610.33845v1 - Build, Don't Play: Self-Improving LLM Research Agents Develop a Hearthstone Battlegrounds AI That Rivals Top Players

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-24T18:33:51Z
- authors: Changlian Ma, Limin Wang
- query-set: cs.MA

Can LLM agents carry a hard real-world problem from raw data to a system that holds its own against people? We study this in Hearthstone Battlegrounds, an eight-player auto-battler with stochastic shops and battles, hidden information and nearly a thousand interacting cards. We present AutoTavern, a self-improving research system: specialised LLM research agents share a typed research memory, every claim passes verification gates, and live games close the loop, so the agents improve the simulator, opponents and policy and, from the same feedback, their own research process; a human sets goals and rules on ambiguous mechanics. In 20 days AutoTavern built TavernGym, the first faithful, full-content reproduction of the live game, and delivered 23 versions of TavernAgent, trained with PPO against opponents calibrated to real games. TavernAgent plays complete games on the live ladder by itself: its rating rose from 6,000 to about 15,000 and, on the public leaderboard's measure, its last 20 records average a placement of 1.60 with 80% first places, better than the server's ten highest-rated players on every statistic (2.34, 59%). TavernBench, 960 tasks in 40 scenarios, shows that the system the agents built is the strongest player: TavernAgent answers 91% of the play tasks against at most 63% for frontier LLMs, and decides in 0.03 s where they need up to 26 s; within the second a live turn allows, it keeps 90% and no LLM more than 26%. LLM agents build far better than they play. We will open-source TavernBench and TavernGym at https://github.com/MCG-NJU/AutoTavern.


## arXiv 2610.33450v1 - Secure and Functional Code Generation with Self-Evaluation

- categories: cs.CR, cs.MA, cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-24T05:02:28Z
- authors: Md Afif Al Mamun, Gias Uddin
- query-set: cs.MA

Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate functionally correct code that remains insecure. Security-focused fine-tuning can be expensive and is not always available, motivating inference-time approaches. Security-oriented prompting and retrieval can reduce vulnerabilities, but may also remove required behavior or apply defenses incompatible with the task. The challenge is to identify relevant security knowledge and use it to improve security without sacrificing functionality. We present SecGent, an inference-time framework that retrieves weakness context from the Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) database and applies it to secure code generation. By connecting task specifications to relevant textual CWE records, SecGent provides task-specific security guidance without relying on retrieved vulnerable or patched code examples. Complementary functional and security tests check the resulting implementation, and their execution feedback guides repairs toward satisfying both objectives. We evaluate SecGent with four open-source LLMs and Claude Sonnet 5.5 across five programming languages on the CWEval benchmark. It achieves the highest joint functional--security success against four inference-time baselines for all five models. Across the open-weight models, mean functional-security@1 (FS@1) improves by 15.5% over RAG, with gains in both functionality and security, and by 19.8% over the strongest prompt optimizer compiled for each model. On Sonnet, FS@1 reaches 92.1%, outperforming the strongest baseline AutoGen by 4.8%. In a separate comparison, SecGent outperforms Claude Code with security-focused prompting by over 6% in FS@1. Feedback-guided repair further improves FS@1 by up to 6.7% over generation without feedback, primarily by recovering functionality while checking security.


## arXiv 2610.33411v1 - Agent-to-Agent Synthetic Social Engineering on the Web

- categories: cs.CR, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-24T02:59:12Z
- authors: Bryan C. Boots
- query-set: cs.MA

An AI agent can prepare an accurate report and still request an action its user has not authorized. Cooperation across Web services therefore requires separate judgments about identity, evidentiary support, and permission. We define synthetic social engineering (SSE) as deliberately deceptive or misleading influence intended to exploit AI recipients' responses to credibility, authority, or obligation cues and redirect behavior away from authorized objectives. We focus on separately tasked sender agents that produce or adapt misleading communication. Drawing on cybersecurity, social engineering, and persuasion research, we develop an operational framework connecting actors, deceptive representations, candidate influence routes, evidence or permission boundaries, responses, and consequences. The framework distinguishes attempts from successful redirection, without assuming human psychological processes. We implement it in the Agent Service Exchange and Evidence Commons (ASE-EC), a reproducible testbed with independently specified records and authorization scopes. Across 1,260 research episodes using Qwen3-4B in two fictional briefing tasks, matched comparisons test authority and commitment appeals, while defense comparisons assess protection alongside authorized task completion. Invoking a limited prior agreement increased unauthorized requests from 43.3% to 96.7%, although recipients retained supported decisions and permissions remained unchanged. These controlled findings identify action selection as a distinct security concern, even when evidence handling succeeds, and they provide a reusable basis for evaluating safeguards that preserve useful cooperation across Web services.


## arXiv 2610.33282v1 - Estimation of Propagated Risk in LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-23T20:46:33Z
- authors: Shreyan Ganguly, Souradeep Chattopadhyay, Qisai Liu, Soumik Sarkar
- query-set: cs.MA

Large Language Model (LLM) based multi-agent systems introduce a distinctive reliability challenge in which actions taken by upstream agents can affect the correctness of downstream agents. While prior work has investigated error propagation and developed methods for estimating uncertainty inherited from upstream agents, existing approaches do not explicitly model how realized sender-receiver interactions contribute to downstream error risk under different sender correctness states. In this work, we study agent-level error-risk estimation from multi-agent execution traces. We propose Interaction-Effect Propagation (IE-Prop), an interaction-conditioned model that learns separate effects for correct and incorrect upstream agents. For each sender-receiver interaction, the model combines these conditional effects using the sender's estimated error risk, and recursively propagates the resulting risk estimates through the execution graph. We evaluate our approach across three multi-agent architectures and three benchmarks. Across all evaluated architectures, our approach raises the final-node AUROC over the established baseline by approximately 0.05 points on average. Furthermore, tracking risk across multiple agent steps contributes a distinct +0.03 AUROC gain in sequential systems. These results show that realized inter-agent interactions provide predictive information beyond receiver-local evidence, while the value of multi-step risk tracking depends on the system's information-flow structure.


## arXiv 2610.33281v1 - Coral Discovery: The Division of Cognitive Labor in Autonomous Research

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-23T20:46:11Z
- authors: Xinxing Ren, Qianbo Zang, Ziyan Wang, Suman Deb, Qiang Yang
- query-set: cs.MA

A research community is only as strong as the way it divides its cognitive labour. The philosopher Philip Kitcher argued that researchers should not all pursue the most promising approach. Credit rules, not a central plan, keep some of them on other lines. We bring this question to autonomous research, where Large Language Model agents improve programs against a deterministic grader. Agents that share only a score follow the leader and converge onto one approach. Coral Discovery gives the community a direction board. A verifier labels every attempt with a direction, the mechanism earning the score, and a method, one change within it. Per direction, the board tracks how much each eval improves the score, adds an exploration bonus for little-tried directions, prices the option of opening a new direction, and suggests how many agents each direction deserves. Credit is earned against a direction's own best, so a lagging direction keeps its agents. Agents finish their lines, then treat the board as advice. We test on nine tasks with the same model and budget, without grader access except for Kernel. Coral Discovery beats the strongest open autonomous multi-agent system on 6 tasks, ties on 1, and loses on 2. Against published numbers, it is best or tied on 4. It improves the reported Kernel result from 1103 to 1095 cycles and passes 1103 at eval 275, under half of the 596 used before. It sets a new Polyominoes state of the art, 91.78 against 87.0. Direction labels show that the leading scores rest on directions a score-only community never entered. Code will be released shortly.


## arXiv 2610.33271v1 - Neuromorphic Decision Dynamics for Deadlock-free Safe Navigation in Congested Environments

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-23T20:32:42Z
- authors: Himani Sinhmar, Vaibhav Srivastava, Naomi Ehrich Leonard
- query-set: cs.MA

Safe navigation in congested environments requires both collision avoidance and coordinated passage. We introduce neuromorphic decision dynamics (NDD), a decentralized, event-based controller, that provides navigation in congested spaces with guarantees on safety, liveness, robustness, and scalability. NDD makes safe-and-live deconfliction a controllable, locally tunable emergent property of local agent interactions, without requiring communication, preassigned priorities, models of neighbors' objectives or future actions, or estimates of group size or global network properties. By design, NDD couples the dynamics of internal decision and attention states with changing physical states and locally observed relative motion. Each agent continuously modulates its speed along its prescribed path, responding as soon and as much as conflict resolution requires. Agents self-organize into disjoint, locally engaged groups that resolve conflicts concurrently. When any group's conflict becomes safety-critical, decision-action feedback destabilizes collective indecision, and coordinated conflict-resolution emerges that stabilizes a safe traversal order within the group. Order persists as conflicts clear, but groups may split or merge as agents autonomously adapt to new conflicts and those with resolved conflicts resume their nominal motion. We derive explicit conditions on sensing range, feedback gains, response time scales, and actuation limits that guarantee collision avoidance and traversal-order formation in finite time with no deadlock or livelock for finite, heterogeneous agent populations of arbitrary, changing size. Computational cost scales with the number of local interactions sensed by each agent. Large-scale simulations, comparisons with existing approaches, and hardware experiments demonstrate NDD in complex congested scenarios and validate its scalability.


## arXiv 2610.32874v1 - Do "True" and "False" Add Up to One? Complement Consistency in LLM-Agent Opinion Simulations

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA, cs.SI
- published: 2026-10-23T16:44:20Z
- authors: Anouar Imel, Jin Qi
- query-set: cs.MA

In LLM-agent opinion simulations, each agent reports the probability that a claim is true after seeing its own previous probability and the probabilities reported by its neighbors, and that number becomes its state in the next round. If the number describes the agent's position rather than the sentence it was shown, then rewriting the claim as its complement and replacing every probability p with 1 - p should return the complementary answer, so the two reports should sum to one. We test this with three small open models: Qwen3-4B, Llama-3.2-3B and Gemma-3-4B. On 2,304 agent states taken from a running network simulation, Qwen's paired reports sum to 1.18 on average, and on 48 real fact-checking claims from AVeriTeC they again exceed one. A controlled benchmark of 24 worlds, each with exactly two possible outcomes, shows the same failure in all three models. The discrepancy is two to ten times larger than the change produced by a same-meaning paraphrase, and it persists under repeated sampling and across peer group sizes and report strengths. When the reports are fed back into two populations that start from complementary states, the populations do not remain mirror images: their divergence is positive after five rounds for every model. Averaging each answer with the complement of its paired answer restores consistency by construction and lowers Brier loss averaged over the two wordings, but at the final round it beats the better of the two original wordings for only one of the three models. A schedule-by-targeting experiment on the same model gives a small, stable interaction because such sweeps keep the claim wording fixed. Consistency under rewording therefore needs its own test before simulated probabilities are read as beliefs


## arXiv 2610.32871v1 - J-Comm: Jacobian-Guided Latent Communication between LLM Agents

- categories: cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-23T16:42:38Z
- authors: Xiangwei Wang, Wei Wang, Sachith Seneviratne, Ken Chen, Yu Xia
- query-set: cs.MA

Latent communication requires both an effective continuous reasoning process and an interface through which an external model can leverage its intermediate computation. We present J-Comm, a framework comprising two core components. First, a training-free Geometric Agreement for Latent Reasoning (GEAR) reweights token candidates over existing model embeddings, which monotonically optimizes geometric consensus among candidate tokens in embedding space at each step. Second, we introduce an H+J (Hidden-and-Jacobian) communication interface that gives the Receiver two complementary views of the Sender's reasoning. The H channel supplies continuous inputs derived from feedback distributions, while the J channel conveys intermediate-state cues in vocabulary-indexed Jacobian-lens coordinates. Intuitively, H provides inputs for the Receiver to reason from, while J offers additional guidance within its computation. The Receiver reconstructs these cues using its own dictionary and injects them into intermediate layers during prefill, enabling communication without sharing key-value caches or requiring identical model widths. In Qwen3-8B to Qwen3-4B communication, J-Comm achieves the highest mean score across seven benchmarks, including 94.80% on MATH500, 54.55% on GPQA-Diamond, 73.33% on AIME2024+2025, and 62.93% success on ALFWorld. Compared with TextMAS, it improves performance by 1.29-8.59 percentage points and reduces generated text tokens by 19.2-74.8%.


## arXiv 2610.32698v1 - Agent-Based Transport Model Calibration via Online Stochastic Batch Optimization

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-23T14:23:36Z
- authors: Abdelkader Dib, Milos Balac
- query-set: cs.MA

Calibrating agent-based models (ABMs) is computationally demanding, as traditional black-box optimization typically requires hundreds or thousands of independent simulation runs. To reduce this burden, we introduce an online calibration framework that estimates parameters within a single simulation run. The approach combines black-box optimization with stochastic batch optimization, a method widely applied in machine learning but not yet explored in ABM calibration. Using MATSim as a case study, we treat each simulation iteration as a stochastic batch, enabling incremental parameter updates as agents make decisions. This online calibration paradigm allows model parameters and agent behaviors to co-evolve, achieving convergence without restarting the simulation. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method by calibrating utility function parameters in MATSim for a large-scale transportation scenario of Switzerland. Results show that mode shares align closely with observed data within a typical simulation timeframe, confirming the feasibility and efficiency of the approach. By embedding calibration directly into the simulation loop, our method opens new possibilities for real-time, scalable, and efficient calibration of complex ABMs.


## arXiv 2610.32554v1 - HGWM: Hierarchical Graph-Guided World Model for Zero-Shot Object Navigation via Graph Matching

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-23T12:35:26Z
- authors: I-tak Ieong, Hao Tang
- query-set: cs.MA

Zero-shot object navigation requires an embodied agent to choose where to search while retaining evidence from previous observations. HGWM couples an LLM-generated room-anchor prior with persistent spatial-semantic memory. A coarse stage assigns visual-semantic scores to candidate headings on a 0-10 scale; a fine stage measures symbolic compatibility between observed objects, room context, and the goal prior. Phase-dependent fusion combines these scores with an LLM direction recommendation before local action selection. On HM3D v0.1, the reported HGWM configuration achieves 62.6% success rate (SR) and 31.5% success weighted by path length (SPL), compared with 58.1% and 31.2% for WMNav. On MP3D, the corresponding values are 46.8% and 17.9%, compared with 45.4% and 17.2%. The results indicate higher success point estimates and modest path-efficiency gains. On HM3D v0.2, the full model obtains 74.4% SR and 31.9% SPL. Additional component ablations reduce SR by 0.9-2.3 percentage points and SPL by 0.6-1.5 points. These are author-reported aggregate measurements. The accompanying implementation repairs pass offline tests; episode-level rerun records are not available. Evaluation is confined to simulation.


## arXiv 2610.32392v1 - PULSES: multi-agent AI for autonomous excited-state model building and dynamics

- categories: physics.chem-ph, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-23T09:17:05Z
- authors: Fangning Ren, Lechen Dong, Xu Chen, Weilin Zou, Bushra Alam
- query-set: cs.MA

Simulating molecular excited-state processes requires resolving electronic and nuclear dynamics, but the cost of direct quantum-chemical calculations limits accessible system sizes and timescales. Machine-learning models can extend these simulations, yet they often need to be tailored to specific molecular systems and scientific objectives. Building and deploying such models requires coordinated decisions across conformational sampling, quantum-chemical labelling, model training, validation and dynamics. Here we introduce Paired agents for Unified Learning and Simulation of Excited States (PULSES), a multi-agent system that automates this workflow within expert-approved boundaries. A human expert and planner define the scientific objective, methods and decision boundaries, while an orchestrator coordinates domain agents and artifact-specific scientific assurance during execution. We evaluate PULSES on 15 computational chemistry tasks and demonstrate end-to-end applications to perylene exciton Hamiltonians, solvent-dependent biphenyl relaxation, and excited-state proton transfer in 10-hydroxybenzo[h]quinoline. These results demonstrate traceable, expert-bounded construction and deployment of specialized excited-state models, while retaining scientific validation as a requirement rather than assuming that successful execution establishes correctness.


## arXiv 2610.32276v1 - Empirically Calibrated Differential Games for Simulating Driver Interactions

- categories: cs.MA, math.OC
- published: 2026-10-23T07:33:58Z
- authors: Amirmohammad Khakpour, Nachuan Li, Pedram Beigi,  Yu,  Nie
- query-set: cs.MA

Game-theoretic models can resolve vehicle interactions without hand-written priority rules, but whether they describe human drivers once calibrated has rarely been tested. This study formulates a general-sum differential game of vehicle interactions at urban intersections, calibrates it to observed driving, and evaluates it on data not used for calibration. Each vehicle minimizes a cost that combines speed tracking, adherence to a map-derived lane route, control effort, gap keeping based on the intelligent driver model, a predictive conflict term, and a contact barrier, while perceiving the other vehicles with a delay. The game is solved with a feedback iterative linear-quadratic algorithm, and ten behavioral parameters per vehicle are calibrated by Bayesian optimization on trajectories recorded at four intersections in Washington, D.C. The calibrated game reproduces the trajectories of 819 vehicles with a mean position error of 1.00 m. When only each vehicle's route is known, as in a simulator, it is the most accurate of the compared models on 96 held-out vehicles (7.42 m), and ten-fold cross-validation shows that it is significantly more accurate than calibrated SUMO (by 3.61 m), an inverse reinforcement learning model (by 0.93 m), and independent and cooperative variants of the same model (by 0.33 and 0.32 m). The solutions, computed in 2.1 s for a typical interaction, are verified approximate equilibria. Finally, the conflict and steering weights, which the data do not identify, can be set from safety and comfort targets; together with a stiffer contact barrier, this removes the collisions that otherwise occur in 43% of tight closed-loop conflicts.


## arXiv 2610.32190v1 - Markets of Minds: A 51-Billion-State Economy of Intelligence

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-23T05:36:29Z
- authors: Jusheng Zhang, Xuekun Wu, Wenhao Wang, Mark Wang, Ming-Hsuan Yang
- query-set: cs.MA

When intelligence is shared, reasoning becomes a constrained allocation problem. In a population of agents with limited access to expert models, an intervention can benefit its recipient yet still be a poor use of shared capacity if another feasible intervention has greater expected value. Assistance must therefore be evaluated through both its marginal benefit and the opportunity cost induced by budgets, access, scarcity, and service capacity. To this end, we introduce Markets of Minds, a framework for allocating expert reasoning across agents before expert outputs are observed. For each feasible agent-service pair, the framework estimates the expected gain from assistance relative to the agent's default continuation, explicitly separating repair benefit from regression risk, and jointly clears assistance contracts under shared resource constraints. We then study this problem in a dependency-aware agent economy with 51.2 billion addressable persona-market states spanning capabilities, cognitive budgets, provider access, scarcity, and network roles. These dependencies couple allocation feasibility across agents rather than the outcome of a fixed contract. Empirically, in a matched replay of 240 cases under a common 30% assistance quota, the frozen allocator reaches 85.42% task-level persona adherence versus 73.86% for equal-capacity random allocation, a gain of 11.56 percentage points. The results show that assistance value is heterogeneous and can be negative, and that scarcity changes which interventions are worth purchasing. Together, these findings motivate a population-level view of intelligence where the value of assistance depends not only on what it can achieve, but also on which alternative uses of scarce cognitive capacity it displaces.


## arXiv 2610.31985v1 - The Helpful Agent Problem: When Emergent Cooperation Among LLM Agents Bypasses Intended Workflows

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-22T22:55:40Z
- authors: Moumita Choudhury, Amit Sarker, Shlomo Zilberstein
- query-set: cs.MA

LLM agents are increasingly deployed in collaborative settings where they pursue separate tasks while sharing resources and operating under review or oversight processes. Such settings create safety risks not only when agents are malicious, but also when they are simply trying to help. Prior work has largely studied adversarial or explicitly collusive behavior; we show that benign agents pursuing separate objectives can spontaneously cooperate in ways that bypass intended workflows. We call this the \emph{helpful agent problem}. We study long-horizon shared-resource environments in which agents sometimes depend on resources owned by their peers and cross-owner changes are normally mediated by a reviewer. When review becomes unavailable, agents begin making changes on one another's behalf despite having no shared reward or explicit incentive to cooperate. Crucially, these actions remain within each agent's local permissions while circumventing the system's intended oversight structure. We introduce \textsc{FavorBench}, a controlled benchmark with measurable downstream side effects, and adapt \textsc{CooperBench} to test the phenomenon in realistic repositories. Across two benchmarks and four models, we find that out-of-workflow favors emerge frequently. Further, in \textsc{FavorBench}, favors from at least three models cause significant harm to parties outside the interaction. These results reveal a distinct multi-agent safety challenge: cooperation itself can create pathways around system-level safeguards.


## arXiv 2610.31974v1 - Agentic AI Defense: Decode-Time Provenance Binding for MCP

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-22T22:27:13Z
- authors: Eric Yocam, Varghese Vaidyan, Paris Kalathas, Patrick Tague, Bruce DeBruhl
- query-set: cs.MA

Agentic AI systems using the Model Context Protocol (MCP) are vulnerable to indirect prompt injection through untrusted tool outputs. We demonstrate cross-modal trust laundering, in which instructions concealed in an email-signature image using least-significant-bit (LSB), discrete cosine transform (DCT), or metadata steganography bypass text scanners and enter a multi-agent workflow when decoded by a tool. To counter this threat, we propose decode-time provenance binding, which combines HMAC-authenticated trust labels with an MCP-maintained provenance ledger and context-taint tracking. The mechanism labels decoded content as untrusted and blocks critical tool calls influenced by that content, even when a compromised agent substitutes a valid trusted label. We evaluate the approach using a real MCP testbed with three steganographic carriers and 24 adaptive payloads. The undefended pipeline exhibits 100% attack success, while keyword and regex defenses each permit 62.5% of attacks. Our mechanism blocks all attacks (0% success), including adversarial label substitution across mediated agent boundaries. A four-component ablation validates the roles of decode marking, cryptographic authentication, lineage tracking, and sink enforcement. An audited trusted-promotion mechanism increases legitimate-task completion from 67% to 100% without compromising protection. The approach provides carrier-independent containment within fully mediated MCP execution environments. Code and data are publicly available.


## arXiv 2610.31971v1 - Knowing Whom to Trust is not Enough: The Trust--Agreement Gap in Repeated Multi-Party LLM Negotiation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-22T22:24:10Z
- authors: Amit Sarker, Moumita Choudhury, Saaduddin Mahmud, Shlomo Zilberstein
- query-set: cs.MA

Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly participate in negotiations on behalf of individuals and organizations, yet they remain vulnerable to manipulation, particularly in repeated multi-party interactions. Classical multi-agent systems address this challenge through explicit trust and reputation models, whereas existing work on LLM negotiation largely treats trust as an emergent phenomenon. We investigate whether LLM agents can form useful trust estimates from prior interactions and leverage them to improve future negotiation outcomes. We introduce a repeated, multi-party, multi-issue negotiation framework in which recurring agents participate in a sequence of negotiation games while one participant covertly attempts to prevent agreement. After each game, agents assess their counterparts and maintain pairwise trust estimates that carry over to subsequent negotiations. We compare trust-aware and trust-agnostic agents and analyze how explicit trust influences negotiation outcomes. Our results reveal a \emph{trust--agreement gap}: trust-aware agents can identify the adversary within a few games, but accurate trust estimates do not consistently translate into better agreements. Trust increases agreement rates by up to 23 percentage points, but only when agents are explicitly guided to use it in their decisions. Explicit trust can therefore improve repeated LLM negotiations, but its value depends critically on how trust is assessed and incorporated into agents' reasoning.


## arXiv 2610.31900v2 - Distributed Swarm Morphogenesis with Neural Cellular Automata

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-22T20:52:51Z
- authors: Ray Verma, Eliseo Ferrante, Anthony Tzes
- query-set: cs.MA

Swarm morphogenesis requires robots to assemble desired shapes through local interactions. We present a modular framework integrating neural cellular automata (NCA) with existing site assignment and collision-aware control. Within this framework, we introduce a decentralized attachment-site discovery mechanism. Robots already in the formation update and share local NCA states to identify new attachment sites, enabling progressive growth without a predefined list of sites during deployment. To bridge the mismatch between conventional NCA growth and gradual physical assembly, we additionally propose offline sparse-retention fine-tuning that simulates partial attachment, together with a false-positive loss that penalizes invalid site activations. Simulations demonstrate formation of diverse lattice morphologies, scaling to several hundred robots, and substantial improvements in morphology accuracy from the proposed fine-tuning. Experiments with Crazyflie quadrotors validate the integrated execution pipeline using robot-relative observations reconstructed from external tracking.


## arXiv 2610.31868v1 - Emergent Cooperative Thermoregulation in Lunar Swarm Robotics

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-22T19:57:47Z
- authors: Anais Jeger, Harry Holt, Lennart Puck
- query-set: cs.MA

Swarms of small robots are an attractive way to survey lunar caves and lava tubes. Missions such as NASA's CADRE will fly cooperating rovers for distributed mapping, but only in daylight. The 354 h lunar night drops the surface towards 95 K, and the radioisotope heaters that let single rovers survive it cannot be supplied at swarm scale. In this study we investigate whether a swarm can instead use its own members as thermal shielding. We train one policy, shared by all twenty robots, by reinforcement learning with an evolution-strategies estimator. The policy receives a single reward, once, at the end of the night: the mean temperature of the five coldest robots. Nothing in that reward refers to position, spacing, exposure or motion. Two behaviors nonetheless emerge. The swarm aggregates, and robots circulate between the sheltered core and the exposed rim, the same pair of behaviors with which emperor penguins survive the Antarctic winter. We measure the survival threshold, the smallest stored energy per robot at which every robot is still alive at sunrise. Across ten runs it is 155.6 +/- 1.3 pcm-h, where one pcm-h is 7.2 kJ of stored heat. That is 37% below a motionless huddle and within 13% of a physics-based lower bound, and at a budget where the motionless huddle loses six robots, every robot survives. The behavior needs no shared spatial reference and no robot-robot contact, and transfers from twenty robots to forty without retraining.


## arXiv 2610.33106v1 - VibeGame: Prompt-to-Game Development with AI-Native Engine and Self-Evolving Adversarial Agent Team

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-22T18:03:17Z
- authors: Wenbo Hu, Ken Li, Jiazhe Wei, Yukang Cao, Weiyi Hong

Large language model agents have enabled software development through natural-language interaction, but extending this paradigm to end-to-end game development remains challenging. Building a playable game requires coordinating code, scenes, assets, animations, physics, and configurations, while allowing developers to validate their integrated behavior through interactive testing. We introduce VibeGame, a framework for creating complete 2D game projects from natural-language prompts and optional reference images. Its AI-native game engine represents project state as schema-validated structured text and provides frame-synchronous runtime control, enabling agents to inspect game states, execute actions, and verify behavior at their own pace. Built upon this interface, we design an Adversarial Agent Team of eight specialized agents to separate implementation from review, so that problems are found and fixed through multi-round loops. For each conducted and accepted project, we further introduce a training-free self-evolution mechanism to distill it into reusable skeletons, modules, and collaboration contracts for future development. We evaluate prompt-assets-to-game (PA2G), where agents are given an asset library, and prompt-to-game (P2G), where agents generate their own art. On a GameCraft-Bench subset, VibeGame in P2G consistently outperforms a range of agent harnesses in either P2G or PA2G with the same underlying model, including GPT-6.1-Sol.


## arXiv 2610.31746v1 - Toward an Agentic AI Approach to Public Health Data Collection

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-22T17:55:56Z
- authors: Xueyi Zhang, Hongru Du, Ensheng Dong
- query-set: cs.MA

Disease surveillance increasingly integrates automated data collection, yet predefined sources and procedures require manual adaptation as reporting practices change. We explore agentic artificial intelligence (AI) as an approach to coordinating surveillance data collection across heterogeneous sources. Specifically, we built a multi-agent framework that integrates source discovery, source assessment, evidence structuring, event linking, and validation. Three retrospective applications illustrate its promise. The workflow recovered all 13 individual cases in a hantavirus outbreak, with links to 85 distinct publisher domains compared with 28 in the reference benchmark, reconstructed Canadian measles counts with a final total 0.33% below the national reference and higher cumulative counts on 79.8% of comparable days, and increased the share of days with recent Sierra Leone mpox reports from 28.2% to 65.6% by incorporating attributed media. These demonstrations also reveal limitations in record completeness and accuracy, while prospective gains in timeliness remain untested. We envision agentic AI as a complementary layer of public health data infrastructure, coordinating evidence-linked collection under surveillance goals and standards defined by public health professionals.


## arXiv 2610.34298v1 - Matched Capabilities, Different Behaviors: A Controlled Study of MCP and CLI Interfaces for Language Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-25T13:54:07Z
- authors: Zhenning Shi, Liang Pang, Yongqi Li
- query-set: MCP

Language agents increasingly act through external tools and interactive environments, making the interface between the model and its executable capabilities part of the decision process. Model Context Protocol (MCP) and command-line interfaces (CLI) can expose the same semantic operations through markedly different model-facing representations. We study whether these representational differences change agent behavior when executable capabilities are matched. We introduce Matched Interface Comparison and Analysis (MICA), which renders MCP and CLI from a shared semantic action contract and routes decoded actions through the same runtime. Across six models and three interactive environments, matched interfaces produce substantial differences in task performance and interaction cost. These effects extend beyond surface validity to semantic action selection, failure pathways, and recovery. Matched-state probes localize sensitivity to capability presentation, realization, and feedback. Paired natural trajectories reveal structured divergence in 94.3% of 2,004 episodes and show that early differences propagate through subsequent interaction. This analysis identifies interface realization as a direct intervention boundary. We therefore introduce Decoupled Action Realization and Targeted Repair (DART), which preserves validated semantic commitments while repairing interface-specific realization. DART improves benchmark score in all six evaluated settings by up to 9.0 points and reduces MCP token cost in all three environments by up to 27.1k tokens per task. Together, these results establish interface representation as part of the closed-loop decision environment shaping agent behavior.


## arXiv 2610.33636v1 - Build or Adopt? Custom-Built vs. General-Purpose LLM Agent Harnesses for Automotive GUI Testing

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-24T12:32:16Z
- authors: Arsham Gholamzadeh Khoee, Ruyu Fu, Jiaqi Wei, Ruikun Deng, Robert Feldt
- query-set: MCP

As general-purpose LLM agent harnesses mature, organizations must decide whether to continue developing in-house systems or adopt existing alternatives. We study this decision at Volvo Group for GUI testing of Android-based in-vehicle infotainment (IVI) systems. We compare a production, custom-built harness with two general-purpose harnesses, Claude Code and OpenCode, keeping the tools, accessed through the Model Context Protocol (MCP), and the models the same. We evaluate the harnesses with up to four models on Volvo's IVI test cases and on open-ended tasks in public apps (Google Maps, Booking.com), and interview ten practitioners: developers, the product owner, and testers. On Volvo's stepwise IVI tests, general-purpose harnesses match the custom-built harness in success but vary more across runs. Their inference cost per task is 2.6-10.7 times higher, yet the engineering effort they save outweighs this: for a nightly IVI regression suite, total monthly cost, including engineering effort and inference, is 3-66% lower. On open-ended public-app tasks, they succeed more often, mainly because they recover better from incorrect actions. Interviews suggest that external advances can turn an in-house harness into technical debt even without deterioration. Adopting a general-purpose harness shifts maintenance from the agent loop to tools and instructions, but gives teams less control over what the model receives. Regardless of the harness, testers want to inspect the agent's actions and ensure that it follows the prescribed test scenario.


## arXiv 2610.33350v1 - EVM-Anchored Provenance for AI-Assisted Code Evolution: A Smoke-Scale Deployment Report

- categories: cs.SE
- published: 2026-10-23T22:39:16Z
- authors: Einar César Santos
- query-set: MCP

We report a smoke-scale deployment of an EVM-anchored provenance framework for the state stream produced by AI-assisted code generation. The system comprises codebase-state-manager, an open-source Model Context Protocol (MCP) server (MIT, v0.2.1) that exposes codebase state, transition, and reward operations to any MCP-compatible assistant, and CodebaseStateRegistry, a Solidity contract deployed on Base Sepolia that emits per-state commitment events. Payloads are pinned to the InterPlanetary File System (IPFS) and indexed through a rebuildable SQLite mirror that resynchronizes by replaying contract events. We delimit precisely what anchoring does and does not attest (existence, ordering, and non-equivocation by the committer key; never truthfulness or fabrication-prevention) and outline the orthogonal supply-chain controls required to close that gap. Under an author-produced synthetic workload on a local Anvil simulator, the median commit costs 103,282 gas, modeled at US$0.0018-0.0035 in rollup fees per commit (US$17.9-34.8 per 10,000 states), and simulator latency for the create operation is 539.8 ms (p50) and 725.0 ms (p95) under loopback storage and instant mining. The live deployment holds 14 StateCommitted events verifiable on-chain; the last consumed 98,977 gas at 1 gwei (about US$0.30). All empirical figures are descriptive quantiles from a single author run; no testnet latency is reported, and no claim of superiority is made against any baseline. A receipts-trie consumption mechanism, a BountyEscrow use case, and a FAIR-aligned dataset publication pattern are specified as outlook and are not implemented. A property-by-property comparison against OpenTimestamps, Sigstore Rekor, Centrifuge AnchorRegistry, in-toto/SLSA, Radicle, and Lalli et al. places this work relative to the surrounding provenance landscape.


## arXiv 2610.32555v1 - Tool-Calling LLM Agents Under Realistic MCP Faults: Scale Does Not Purchase Fault Tolerance

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-23T12:35:51Z
- authors: Antonio Ken Iannillo, Joshua Segun Owotogbe, Roberto Natella, Francesco Avallone, Kristina Kudryavtseva
- query-set: MCP

LLM agents increasingly rely on external tools exposed via the Model Context Protocol (MCP), yet current benchmarks assume tools respond correctly and on time, leaving agent resilience to realistic tool failures untested. We introduce MCP-Injector, a drop-in, protocol-aware fault-injection middleware that interposes on the MCP tool-call path with controlled persistence (permanent, transient, intermittent) and seedable schedules. Its fault model is calibrated to the observed incident distribution (failure mode x persistence) of the public MCP ecosystem, mined from 57,240 issue and pull-request threads across 1,774 open-source MCP repositories. In a 1,350-cell experiment crossing two backbone sizes (20B, 120B), three agent stacks (custom multi-round plan-and-execute, OpenAI Agents SDK, LangChain), and three fault injection conditions, we find that faults drawn from this permanent-heavy stress profile cause severe quality degradation (task-completion score falls by 60%, from 8.08 to 3.23 out of 10), and that scale does not purchase fault tolerance for the two gpt-oss backbones we test: the 120B retains no larger share of its fault-free score under faults (resilience-ratio model, $p = 0.96$), and its completion rate under faults is, if anything, lower (91.1% vs. 95.6%; difference 4.4 points, 95% CI $[-0.2, 9.3]$). Agent stacks differ in how well they absorb faults, which suggests that framework-level error handling matters more than model scale. These results demonstrate that capability benchmarks alone can mask operational brittleness, motivating protocol-aware fault injection as a complementary evaluation dimension for tool-calling agents.


## arXiv 2610.31974v1 - Agentic AI Defense: Decode-Time Provenance Binding for MCP

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-10-22T22:27:13Z
- authors: Eric Yocam, Varghese Vaidyan, Paris Kalathas, Patrick Tague, Bruce DeBruhl
- query-set: MCP

Agentic AI systems using the Model Context Protocol (MCP) are vulnerable to indirect prompt injection through untrusted tool outputs. We demonstrate cross-modal trust laundering, in which instructions concealed in an email-signature image using least-significant-bit (LSB), discrete cosine transform (DCT), or metadata steganography bypass text scanners and enter a multi-agent workflow when decoded by a tool. To counter this threat, we propose decode-time provenance binding, which combines HMAC-authenticated trust labels with an MCP-maintained provenance ledger and context-taint tracking. The mechanism labels decoded content as untrusted and blocks critical tool calls influenced by that content, even when a compromised agent substitutes a valid trusted label. We evaluate the approach using a real MCP testbed with three steganographic carriers and 24 adaptive payloads. The undefended pipeline exhibits 100% attack success, while keyword and regex defenses each permit 62.5% of attacks. Our mechanism blocks all attacks (0% success), including adversarial label substitution across mediated agent boundaries. A four-component ablation validates the roles of decode marking, cryptographic authentication, lineage tracking, and sink enforcement. An audited trusted-promotion mechanism increases legitimate-task completion from 67% to 100% without compromising protection. The approach provides carrier-independent containment within fully mediated MCP execution environments. Code and data are publicly available.


## arXiv 2610.31113v1 - Can AI Co-Design Structures? An AI Agent for Early-Stage Structural Design

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-22T11:27:22Z
- authors: Ricardo Maia Avelino, Mennatallah El-Assady, Tom Van Mele, Philippe Block
- query-set: MCP

Recent advances in large language models and agentic workflows offer a new paradigm to early-stage structural design translating design intent into structural models. Although text-to-3D approaches have demonstrated that natural language can be used to produce visual, mesh-based geometric models, structural design requires domain-specific geometric arrangements and semantically rich data types. This paper introduces a proof-of-concept workflow for prompt-to-structure generation using an AI agent connected to a COMPAS-based structural modelling API through a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server. Through controlled modelling operations, the agent creates and modifies a graph-based structural data model composed of explicit building elements. The resulting artifacts can be inspected, modified, serialised, and further connected to downstream AEC workflows. The approach is demonstrated through selected prompt-to-structure examples and by progressive and editable design sequences that resemble typical iterative structural design workflows. The results are preliminary, but demonstrate the possibility of combining natural language, agentic workflows, and domain-specific structural data structures as a first step toward AI-assisted structural co-design.


## arXiv 2610.31080v1 - nnInteractive Agent: General-Purpose AI Agents as 3D Image Annotators

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-22T10:55:15Z
- authors: Fabian Isensee, Constantin Ulrich Harsy, Maximilian Rokuss, Benjamin Hamm, Philipp Vollmuth
- query-set: MCP

Interactive 3D segmentation models such as nnInteractive turn a few spatial prompts (points, scribbles, boxes) into accurate volumetric masks, but a human still does the annotation work: finding each structure, placing prompts, checking the result and correcting it. General-purpose AI agents can now interpret images, call tools and write code. We show that such agents can take over this work without additional training: the user makes a request in plain language, and the agent works out what has to be segmented, locates the targets, prompts nnInteractive, inspects the 3D result, corrects it and responds to feedback. Asked for 'the muscles used for chewing', an agent identifies and segments the masseters and pterygoids; asked to count liver lesions, it writes its own code to find candidates and splits a merged lesion on request. Our open-source nninteractive-agent provides nnInteractive as tools over the Model Context Protocol (MCP), together with a skill that encodes how experienced users prompt it. On a benchmark spanning CT, MRI, PET and micro-CT of humans, a mouse and an ant (14 images; 51 targets from organs and tumours to muscles, foot bones, vessel trees and an insect brain), Claude Code and Codex reach a mean Dice of 0.81 and 0.84 from the request alone; when told where each target is, both reach 0.85 and prompt nearly as effectively as an oracle that places prompts using the reference segmentation. A text-prompted 3D model (VoxTell) is more accurate on most standard radiological targets but less robust: it returns an empty mask for one of them and fails on the leg muscles, the foot bones, the mouse and the ant, where the agents remain accurate. Code: https://github.com/MIC-DKFZ/nninteractive-agent


## arXiv 2610.31703v1 - Before the Ticket: Grounding HPC User Support in Documentation and Resolved Tickets with an Agentic LLM Assistant

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-10-21T19:01:41Z
- authors: Elham J. Barezi, Primus C Kabuo, Niveditha Nerella
- query-set: MCP

Most questions that reach an HPC help desk have already been answered in the center's user guides or in a ticket someone else filed last semester; users still file new tickets because the guides are hard to navigate and the ticket history is not theirs to search. We present an assistant that answers from both sources: an orchestrator calls two tools over the Model Context Protocol, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) over the live documentation and a Llama-3-8B model fine-tuned with QLoRA on 11,244 query-resolution pairs mined from historical tickets, and merges their answers. Every model runs on-premises or on the university's hosted LLM service because ticket data may not leave the institution. On 400 held-out tickets from Purdue's Rosen Center for Advanced Computing, disjoint from training and free of near-duplicates, and evaluated by two judges from model families that generated none of the answers, fine-tuning on resolutions rewritten into self-service guidance is the clear winner in agreement with RCAC's resolutions (BERTScore F1 improves from 0.06 to 0.27 over the base model) and is judged most correct on average. The end-to-end system does not improve on its tools, although they are complementary: choosing the better answer per question would exceed either tool (judged correctness 3.41 versus 3.20, an oracle bound), while the merge follows the documentation tool in 78% of answers. Returning one tool's answer instead recovers the fine-tuned tool's quality (3.19 versus 2.66), but neither a learned selector nor an LLM verifier beats that tool alone.


## arXiv 2610.28810v1 - Combining Long-Term Memory and LLMs Comes at a High Cost: From Prompt Injection to Persistent Compromise

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
- published: 2026-10-20T18:33:05Z
- authors: Pedro H. Barcha Correia, Matheus Azevedo de Sá, Paulo H. Gomes de Senna, Giovanni Mandel Martignago, Victor Takashi Hayashi
- query-set: MCP

The widespread adoption of generative artificial intelligence in recent years has brought a parallel rise in prompt injection and jailbreak attacks. By embedding malicious instructions, these exploits manipulate language models to bypass intended behavior and operational boundaries. In traditional chatbots, such attacks are confined to a single session because state memory is not preserved across conversations. However, persistent memory features in modern conversational interfaces enable cross-session attack propagation, establishing a threat vector known as memory injection. This work explores this emerging vulnerability in leading LLM-as-a-service platforms, highlighting its real-world severity and the need for countermeasures. While existing literature focuses on violations tailored to specific platform features, we present a general threat model. We demonstrate that all NIST attack goals are collectively achievable across ChatGPT Instant 5.3, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Gemini 3 Flash under this model. We also show that memory injections can exploit multiple attack vectors, including personal memories, projects, skills, organization instructions, and Model Context Protocol (MCP)-connected resources. Our findings reveal that all evaluated services can be manipulated to persistently spread disinformation, generate harmful content, or simulate offline unresponsiveness, even in fresh sessions. In the particular case of Claude, shared-memory features allow an attacker to compromise multiple users within an organization and also exfiltrate user prompts. To address these vulnerabilities, we derive defense mechanisms from our proposed threat model and foundational cybersecurity principles. Our analysis indicates that nearly half of these mitigations remain unimplemented across the target services. Datasets and attack artifacts are available at https://github.com/PedroBarcha/Memory-injection.


## arXiv 2610.28785v1 - Measure First, Then Allocate: Calibrated Rollout Budgets for MCP Agents in Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.LG, cs.CL
- published: 2026-10-20T18:15:52Z
- authors: Hieu Tran, Zonghai Yao, Sunjae Kwon, Hong Yu
- query-set: MCP

Training tool-using language models requires costly interaction, yet identical rewards within a rollout group provide no group-relative learning signal. We use reward disagreement to guide harness evaluation and the allocation of rollouts for policy training in Model Context Protocol (MCP) environments. We first introduce Discord, which tests harness edits using exact paired inference and decision-preserving early stopping: only disagreeing paired outcomes favor one harness over another. This insight motivates prospective allocation in BRACE (Bank-calibrated Rollout Allocation under Correlated Episodes).
